 \frac{te^{xt}}{\EulerE^t-1}&=\sum_{n\geq0}\beta_n(x)\frac{t^n}{n!},      \label{eq:merged-bernoulli-poly-egf}\\
 \frac{2\EulerE^{xt}}{\EulerE^t+1}&=\sum_{n\geq0}\mathsf E_n(x)\frac{t^n}{n!}.\label{eq:merged-euler-poly-egf}
\end{align}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Appell, translation, and explicit formulas]
\label{thm:merged-appell}
For $n\geq1$,
\begin{align}
 \beta_n'(x)&=n\beta_{n-1}(x),&
 \mathsf E_n'(x)&=n\mathsf E_{n-1}(x),                              \label{eq:merged-appell-derivative}\\
 \beta_n(x+y)&=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom nk\beta_k(x)y^{n-k},&
 \mathsf E_n(x+y)&=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom nk\mathsf E_k(x)y^{n-k},  \label{eq:merged-appell-translation}\\
 \beta_n(x)&=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom nk\beta_kx^{n-k}.                \label{eq:merged-bernoulli-explicit}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Differentiating either polynomial EGF with respect to $x$ multiplies it by
$t$, which proves the derivative identities.  Replacing $x$ by $x+y$
multiplies by $\EulerE^{yt}$, and the exponential Cauchy product gives translation.
Setting $x=0$ in the Bernoulli translation formula gives the explicit
polynomial expansion.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal crosswalk]
% ed.: crosswalk added 2026-09-01.
For the Bernoulli polynomials, the derivative identity in
\cref{eq:merged-appell-derivative} is Mathlib's
\lean{Polynomial.derivative_bernoulli}, and the translation formula
\cref{eq:merged-appell-translation} is \lean{Fabius.bernoulli_eval_add}
(module \lean{BernoulliAppell}).  The formal proof is the Taylor expansion
rather than the generating function: iterating the derivative identity gives
$\beta_n^{(k)}=n^{\underline k}\beta_{n-k}$
(\lean{Fabius.iterate_derivative_bernoulli}), so the Hasse derivatives are
$\binom nk\beta_{n-k}$ (\lean{Fabius.hasseDeriv_bernoulli}), and Mathlib's
\lean{Polynomial.taylor} expands $\beta_n(x+y)=\sum_k\beta_n^{[k]}(x)y^k$.
The explicit formula \cref{eq:merged-bernoulli-explicit} is Mathlib's
definition \lean{Polynomial.bernoulli_def}.  For the Euler polynomials,
\lean{Fabius.derivative_eulerPolynomial} and \lean{Fabius.eulerPolynomial_eval_add}
(module \lean{EulerPolynomials}) are the derivative and translation identities,
the latter an instance of the general Appell lemma \lean{Fabius.appell_eval_add}
(an Appell sequence with $\deg p_n\le n$ satisfies the translation formula, by
the Taylor expansion).
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Difference and reflection identities]
\label{thm:merged-bernoulli-euler-basic}
For all relevant $n$,
\begin{align}
 \beta_n(x+1)-\beta_n(x)&=nx^{n-1} &&(n\geq1),                      \label{eq:merged-bernoulli-difference}\\
 \mathsf E_n(x+1)+\mathsf E_n(x)&=2x^n,                             \label{eq:merged-euler-difference}\\
 \beta_n(1-x)&=(-1)^n\beta_n(x),                                   \label{eq:merged-bernoulli-reflection}\\
 \mathsf E_n(1-x)&=(-1)^n\mathsf E_n(x).                           \label{eq:merged-euler-reflection}
\end{align}
Consequently $\beta_{2m+1}=0$ for $m\geq1$ and
$\mathsf E_{2m+1}(1/2)=0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Subtract the Bernoulli EGFs at $x+1$ and $x$:
\[
 \frac{te^{(x+1)t}-te^{xt}}{\EulerE^t-1}=te^{xt}.
\]
Add the two Euler EGFs to obtain $2\EulerE^{xt}$; coefficient comparison proves the
difference equations.  Replacing $t$ by $-t$ in the Bernoulli EGF gives
\[
 \frac{(-t)\EulerE^{-xt}}{\EulerE^{-t}-1}=\frac{te^{(1-x)t}}{\EulerE^t-1},
\]
and the analogous Euler calculation proves both reflections.  At $x=0$,
$\beta_n(1)=\beta_n$ for $n>1$ by the difference equation; reflection then
forces every odd $\beta_n$ beyond $\beta_1$ to vanish.  Euler reflection at
$x=1/2$ proves the last assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal crosswalk]
% ed.: crosswalk added 2026-09-01.
For the Bernoulli polynomials the difference identity
\cref{eq:merged-bernoulli-difference} is Mathlib's
\lean{Polynomial.bernoulli_eval_one_add}, the reflection
\cref{eq:merged-bernoulli-reflection} is \lean{Polynomial.bernoulli_eval_one_sub},
and $\beta_{2m+1}=0$ is \lean{bernoulli_eq_zero_of_odd}.  For the Euler
polynomials of module \lean{EulerPolynomials} (defined by
\lean{Fabius.eulerPolynomial}, $\mathsf E_n(x)=\sum_k\binom nk\mathsf E_k(0)x^{n-k}$
with $\mathsf E_k(0)$ the coefficients of $2/(\EulerE^t+1)$), the difference
identity \cref{eq:merged-euler-difference} is
\lean{Fabius.eulerPolynomial_eval_add_one_add}, proved as in the text by
comparing the coefficients of $(\EulerE^t+1)\sum_n\mathsf E_n(x)t^n/n!=2\EulerE^{xt}$
(\lean{Fabius.sum_choose_mul_eulerPolynomial_add}) and translating
(\lean{Fabius.eulerPolynomial_eval_add}).  The Euler reflection
\cref{eq:merged-euler-reflection} is \lean{Fabius.eulerPolynomial_eval_one_sub}
(module \lean{EulerReflection}), proved as in the text: the substitutions
$x\mapsto1-x$ and $t\mapsto-t$ turn the generating-function identity into the
same identity $F\,(\EulerE^t+1)=2\EulerE^t\EulerE^{-xt}$, and the unit
$\EulerE^t+1$ is cancelled; $\mathsf E_{2m+1}(1/2)=0$ is
\lean{Fabius.eulerPolynomial_eval_half_odd}.
\end{remark}

\section{Power sums and multiplication formulas}

\begin{theorem}[Faulhaber with an offset]
\label{thm:merged-faulhaber}
For $N,p\in\NaturalNumbers$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j=0}^{N-1}(x+j)^p
 =\frac{\beta_{p+1}(x+N)-\beta_{p+1}(x)}{p+1}.                      \label{eq:merged-faulhaber}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Apply \eqref{eq:merged-bernoulli-difference} to $\beta_{p+1}(x+j)$ and sum;
the left side telescopes.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal crosswalk]
% ed.: crosswalk added 2026-09-01.
\lean{Fabius.sum_range_add_pow_eq_bernoulli_sub} (module \lean{FaulhaberOffset})
is \cref{eq:merged-faulhaber} for all $N\ge0$ and rational $x$, proved by
exactly this telescoping, with the difference identity taken from Mathlib
(\lean{Polynomial.bernoulli_eval_one_add}); the case $x=0$ is also Mathlib's
\lean{Polynomial.sum_range_pow_eq_bernoulli_sub}.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Alternating power sums]
\label{thm:merged-alternating-sums}
For $N,p\in\NaturalNumbers$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j=0}^{N-1}(-1)^j(x+j)^p
 =\frac{\mathsf E_p(x)+(-1)^{N-1}\mathsf E_p(x+N)}2.               \label{eq:merged-alternating-sums}
\end{equation}
The formula gives zero when $N=0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Multiply the Euler difference equation at $x+j$ by $(-1)^j$ and sum.  All
interior Euler terms cancel in pairs, leaving the two endpoints.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal crosswalk]
% ed.: crosswalk added 2026-09-01.
\lean{Fabius.sum_neg_one_pow_mul_pow_eq_eulerPolynomial} (module
\lean{EulerPolynomials}) is \cref{eq:merged-alternating-sums} in the form
$\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}(-1)^j(x+j)^p=\bigl(\mathsf E_p(x)-(-1)^N\mathsf E_p(x+N)\bigr)/2$,
which agrees with the displayed formula for $N\ge1$ and gives $0$ for $N=0$;
the formal proof is the induction on $N$ from the difference identity
\lean{Fabius.eulerPolynomial_eval_add_one_add}.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Raabe multiplication]
\label{thm:merged-raabe}
For every positive integer $q$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{r=0}^{q-1}\beta_n\!\left(x+\frac rq\right)
 =q^{1-n}\beta_n(qx).                                               \label{eq:merged-raabe}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}
 \beta_n\!\left(\frac12\right)=(2^{1-n}-1)\beta_n.                 \label{eq:merged-bernoulli-half}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Sum the generating functions and use the finite geometric series:
\[
 \sum_{r=0}^{q-1}\frac{te^{(x+r/q)t}}{\EulerE^t-1}
 =\frac{te^{xt}}{\EulerE^{t/q}-1}
 =q\frac{(t/q)\EulerE^{(qx)(t/q)}}{\EulerE^{t/q}-1}.
\]
Coefficient comparison gives the multiplication formula.  Set $q=2,x=0$ and
solve for the half-value.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal crosswalk]
% ed.: crosswalk added 2026-09-01; the Lean proof is this proof made formal.
\lean{Fabius.raabe} (module \lean{RaabeMultiplication}) is
\cref{eq:merged-raabe}, written $\sum_{r<q}\beta_n(x+r/q)=q\,(1/q)^n\beta_n(qx)$
for $q\ge1$ and rational $x$.  In $(\RationalNumbers[x])[[t]]$, Mathlib's
\lean{Polynomial.bernoulli_generating_function} gives
$B(a;t)(\EulerE^t-1)=t\EulerE^{at}$ for polynomial arguments $a$
(\lean{Fabius.bernoulliPolySeriesAt_mul_exp_sub_one}); summing over
$a=x+r/q$ and using the finite geometric sum
$\bigl(\sum_{r<q}\EulerE^{rt/q}\bigr)(\EulerE^{t/q}-1)=\EulerE^t-1$
(\lean{Fabius.sum_pow_mul_sub_one}) gives $G(t)(\EulerE^{t/q}-1)=t\EulerE^{xt}$
for the left side; rescaling $B(qx;t)$ by $t\mapsto t/q$
(\lean{Fabius.rescale_expSeries}) gives the same identity for the right side;
and $\EulerE^{t/q}-1$ is $t$ times a unit, which is cancelled.
\cref{eq:merged-bernoulli-half} is \lean{Fabius.bernoulli_eval_half}.
\end{remark}

\section{Stirling and Touchard representations}
\label{sec:merged-bernoulli-stirling-touchard}

The ordinary Bernoulli numbers form a particularly simple signed Stirling
transform.  The same identity has a useful integral form in terms of the
Touchard polynomial
\[
 \TouchardPolynomial{n}(z):=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\StirlingSecondKind nk z^k.
\]
The integral representation below packages the factor $k!/(k+1)$ as a
moment; compare the classical Stirling and exponential-polynomial treatments
in \cite{Comtet1974,Bell1934}.

\begin{theorem}[Bernoulli--Stirling formula and Touchard moments]
\label{thm:merged-bernoulli-stirling-touchard}
For every $n\in\NaturalNumbers$,
\begin{equation}
 \beta_n=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^k\frac{k!}{k+1}\StirlingSecondKind nk .
 \label{eq:merged-bernoulli-stirling}
\end{equation}
Let
\begin{equation}
 E_1(x):=\int_1^\infty \frac{\EulerE^{-xt}}{t}\Differential t
        =\int_x^\infty\frac{\EulerE^{-v}}{v}\Differential v,
 \qquad x>0.                                                       \label{eq:merged-exponential-integral-one}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{align}
 \beta_n
 &=\int_0^1\int_0^\infty
     \EulerE^{-s}\TouchardPolynomial{n}(-us)
     \Differential s\,\Differential u,                            \label{eq:merged-bernoulli-touchard-double-integral}\\
 &=\int_0^\infty E_1(x)\TouchardPolynomial{n}(-x)\Differential x. \label{eq:merged-bernoulli-touchard-integral}
\end{align}
Both integrals are absolutely convergent.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put $y=\EulerE^t-1$.  Since $t=\log(1+y)$, formal composition gives
\[
 \frac{t}{\EulerE^t-1}
 =\frac{\log(1+y)}{y}
 =\sum_{k\geq0}\frac{(-1)^k}{k+1}y^k.
\]
The exponential generating function for Stirling numbers is
\[
 (\EulerE^t-1)^k
 =k!\sum_{n\geq k}\StirlingSecondKind nk\frac{t^n}{n!}.
\]
Substitution and comparison of $t^n/n!$ prove
\eqref{eq:merged-bernoulli-stirling}.  \Cref{thm:merged-bernoulli-difference}
below re-proves it as the value at $x=0$ of a polynomial identity.

Because $\TouchardPolynomial{n}$ is a polynomial, the first integral may be
evaluated term by term:
\begin{align*}
 \int_0^1\int_0^\infty
 \EulerE^{-s}\TouchardPolynomial{n}(-us)\Differential s\,\Differential u
 &=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^k\StirlingSecondKind nk
 \left(\int_0^1u^k\Differential u\right)
 \left(\int_0^\infty s^k\EulerE^{-s}\Differential s\right)\\
 &=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^k\frac{k!}{k+1}\StirlingSecondKind nk.
\end{align*}
Absolute convergence follows from
$|\TouchardPolynomial{n}(-us)|\leq C_n(1+s^n)$ for $0\leq u\leq1$.

For the single integral, Tonelli's theorem gives, for every $k\geq0$,
\begin{align*}
 \int_0^\infty x^kE_1(x)\Differential x
 &=\int_1^\infty\frac{\Differential t}{t}
   \int_0^\infty x^k\EulerE^{-tx}\Differential x
 =k!\int_1^\infty t^{-k-2}\Differential t
 =\frac{k!}{k+1}.
\end{align*}
Termwise integration of the finite Touchard polynomial gives
\eqref{eq:merged-bernoulli-touchard-integral}.  The integral is absolutely
convergent: for $0<x\leq1$,
\[
 E_1(x)\leq\log(1/x)+\int_1^\infty\frac{\EulerE^{-v}}v\Differential v,
\]
whereas $E_1(x)\leq\EulerE^{-x}/x$ for $x\geq1$.
% ed.: crosswalk added 2026-09-01.
\begin{remark}[Formal crosswalk]
\cref{eq:merged-bernoulli-stirling} is \lean{Fabius.bernoulli_eq_sum_stirlingSecond}
(module \lean{BernoulliStirling}) for Mathlib's Bernoulli numbers
\lean{bernoulli}, whose convention $B_1=-1/2$ matches the generating function
$t/(\EulerE^t-1)$ used here.  The Lean proof is the first paragraph of this
proof made formal: Mathlib's \lean{bernoulliPowerSeries_mul_exp_sub_one}
gives $\mathscr B(t)(\EulerE^t-1)=t$, \lean{Fabius.log_subst_exp_sub_one}
gives $t=\log(1+y)$ at $y=\EulerE^t-1$, cancelling the non-zero-divisor
$\EulerE^t-1=t\cdot(\EulerE^t-1)/t$ identifies $\mathscr B$ with
$\log(1+y)/y$ composed with $\EulerE^t-1$
(\lean{Fabius.bernoulliPowerSeries_eq_logDivSeries_subst}), and the
coefficients are read off by the exponential composition theorem
(\lean{Fabius.egfA_subst_bellWeightSeries}) with the Stirling specialization of
the partial Bell polynomials.  The two integral representations are not
formalized.
\end{remark}
\end{proof}

Formula \eqref{eq:merged-bernoulli-stirling} is the specialization at $x=0$ of
a polynomial identity in $x$, which is worth recording separately: it expresses
the whole Bernoulli polynomial in the finite-difference basis, and it makes the
Bernoulli--Stirling formula a corollary of the elementary evaluation
$\Delta^k0^n=k!\StirlingSecondKind nk$.

\begin{theorem}[Bernoulli polynomials from finite differences]
\label{thm:merged-bernoulli-difference}
Let $\Delta$ denote the forward difference in $x$, so that
$\Delta^kx^n=\sum_{j=0}^k(-1)^{k-j}\binom kj(x+j)^n$.  Then for every
$n\in\NaturalNumbers$,
\begin{equation}
 \beta_n(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(-1)^k}{k+1}\Delta^kx^n.           \label{eq:merged-bernoulli-newton-basis}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The proof of \cref{thm:merged-bernoulli-stirling-touchard} already established,
by formal composition of $\log(1+y)/y$ with $y=\EulerE^t-1\in t\RationalNumbers[[t]]$,
that
\begin{equation}
 \frac{t}{\EulerE^t-1}=\sum_{k\geq0}\frac{(-1)^k}{k+1}(\EulerE^t-1)^k
                                                                  \label{eq:merged-gregory-composition}
\end{equation}
in $\RationalNumbers[[t]]$; the composition is legitimate, and the sum
contributes to each power of $t$ only finitely often, because
$(\EulerE^t-1)^k\in t^k\RationalNumbers[[t]]$.  Multiplying
\eqref{eq:merged-gregory-composition} by $\EulerE^{xt}$ in
$\RationalNumbers[x][[t]]$ gives
\[
 \frac{t\EulerE^{xt}}{\EulerE^t-1}
 =\sum_{k\geq0}\frac{(-1)^k}{k+1}\EulerE^{xt}(\EulerE^t-1)^k.
\]
Finally $\EulerE^{xt}(\EulerE^t-1)^k
=\sum_{j=0}^k(-1)^{k-j}\binom kj\EulerE^{(x+j)t}$, whose coefficient of
$t^n/n!$ is $\sum_{j=0}^k(-1)^{k-j}\binom kj(x+j)^n=\Delta^kx^n$.  Comparing
coefficients of $t^n/n!$, and noting that $\Delta^kx^n=0$ for $k>n$ since
$\Delta$ lowers the degree, yields \eqref{eq:merged-bernoulli-newton-basis}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal crosswalk]
% ed.: crosswalk added 2026-09-02; the Lean proof follows the text, with the composition truncated.
\lean{Fabius.bernoulli_eq_sum_fwdDiff} (module \lean{BernoulliNewtonBasis}) is
\cref{eq:merged-bernoulli-newton-basis} as an identity in $\mathbb Q[x]$,
with $\Delta^kx^n$ written out as $\sum_{j=0}^k(-1)^{k-j}\binom kj(x+j)^n$.
The formal proof works in $\mathbb Q[x][[t]]$: Mathlib's
\lean{Polynomial.bernoulli_generating_function} and
\lean{bernoulliPowerSeries_mul_exp_sub_one} give
$\sum_n\beta_n(x)t^n/n!=\EulerE^{xt}\cdot t/(\EulerE^t-1)$
(\lean{Fabius.bernoulliPolySeries_eq}), the composition
\cref{eq:merged-gregory-composition} is used only through its truncation
$t/(\EulerE^t-1)\equiv\sum_{k\le n}\frac{(-1)^k}{k+1}(\EulerE^t-1)^k$ modulo
$t^{n+1}$ (\lean{Fabius.X_pow_dvd_bernoulliPowerSeries_sub_gregory}, whose
coefficients are compared through the Bernoulli--Stirling formula
\lean{Fabius.bernoulli_eq_sum_stirlingSecond} and
$(\EulerE^t-1)^k=k!\sum_nS(n,k)t^n/n!$), and
$\EulerE^{xt}(\EulerE^t-1)^k=\sum_j(-1)^{k-j}\binom kj\EulerE^{(x+j)t}$ is the
binomial theorem for Mathlib's \lean{PowerSeries.exp_mul_exp_eq_exp_add}.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Second proof of the Bernoulli--Stirling formula]
\label{cor:merged-bernoulli-stirling-second-proof}
Evaluating \eqref{eq:merged-bernoulli-newton-basis} at $x=0$ recovers
\eqref{eq:merged-bernoulli-stirling}, by a route independent of the Stirling
exponential generating function used in
\cref{thm:merged-bernoulli-stirling-touchard}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
It suffices to evaluate $\Delta^k0^n$.  By the surjection formula
\cref{thm:second-explicit},
\[
 \Delta^k0^n=\sum_{j=0}^k(-1)^{k-j}\binom kj j^n
 =k!\StirlingSecondKind nk,
\]
this being the inclusion--exclusion count of the surjections
$[n]\twoheadrightarrow[k]$, of which there are $k!\StirlingSecondKind nk$.
Substituting into \eqref{eq:merged-bernoulli-newton-basis} at $x=0$ gives
\[
 \beta_n=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(-1)^k}{k+1}\,k!\StirlingSecondKind nk,
\]
which is \eqref{eq:merged-bernoulli-stirling}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal crosswalk]
% ed.: crosswalk added 2026-09-02.
\lean{Fabius.bernoulli_eq_sum_fwdDiff_zero} (module \lean{BernoulliNewtonBasis})
is the evaluation at $x=0$,
$\beta_n=\sum_{k\le n}\frac{(-1)^k}{k+1}\sum_{j\le k}(-1)^{k-j}\binom kjj^n$,
and the surjection formula $\Delta^k0^n=k!\StirlingSecondKind nk$ is
\lean{Fabius.factorial_mul_stirlingSecond_eq_sum}, so that the two together
are \lean{Fabius.bernoulli_eq_sum_stirlingSecond}.  (In the formal development
the Newton-basis identity itself is derived through the Bernoulli--Stirling
formula, so it is not an independent second proof there.)
\end{remark}

\section{N\"orlund's generalized Bernoulli polynomials}
\label{sec:merged-norlund-polynomials}

The ordinary Bernoulli polynomials sit inside the two-parameter N\"orlund
family \cite{Norlund1924}.  The order parameter makes convolution visible and
turns an isolated exponential diagonal into a polynomial identity.

\begin{definition}[Generalized Bernoulli polynomials]
\label{def:merged-norlund-polynomials}
Let $R$ be a commutative $\RationalNumbers$-algebra, put
$B(t)=t/(\EulerE^t-1)$ with constant coefficient $1$, and write
$L(t)=\log B(t)$ with constant coefficient zero.
For every scalar $\alpha\in R$, define the polynomials
$\beta_n^{(\alpha)}(x)\in R[x]$ by
\begin{equation}
 \left(\frac{t}{\EulerE^t-1}\right)^\alpha\EulerE^{xt}
 =\sum_{n\geq0}\beta_n^{(\alpha)}(x)\frac{t^n}{n!}.                \label{eq:merged-norlund-egf}
\end{equation}
Here the formal power is $B^\alpha=\exp(\alpha L)$, not an independently
chosen branch.  In particular, if
$b_k(\alpha)=k!\CoefficientExtraction tk\exp(\alpha L(t))$, then
\[
 \beta_n^{(\alpha)}(x)
 =\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk b_k(\alpha)x^{n-k}.
\]
This finite formula also defines evaluation at every $x\in R$.
For complex parameters, \eqref{eq:merged-norlund-egf} holds analytically
on $|t|<2\pi$, with the unique holomorphic logarithm normalized by $L(0)=0$.
The order-zero and order-one specializations are
$\beta_n^{(0)}(x)=x^n$ and $\beta_n^{(1)}(x)=\beta_n(x)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proof}[Well-definedness and analytic interpretation]
The series $G(t)=(\EulerE^t-1)/t$ has constant coefficient $1$, so its
formal reciprocal $B$ exists and also has constant coefficient $1$.
The normalized formal logarithm $L$ is therefore defined, and $L(0)=0$
makes every coefficient of $\exp(\alpha L)$ a finite sum.
The exponential Cauchy product gives the displayed polynomial formula.
Its coefficient of $x^n$ is $b_0(\alpha)=1$, so for a nonzero coefficient
ring each polynomial is monic of degree $n$.  Monicity, interpreted as
leading coefficient $1$, also holds in the zero ring, where $0=1$; only
the exact degree assertion needs the nonzero-ring hypothesis.
For $\alpha=0$ the first factor is $1$; for $\alpha=1$ it is
$\exp L=B$.  This proves the two specializations.
More generally, write $U_\alpha=\exp(\alpha L)$.  The formal exponential
product law gives
\[
 U_0=1,\qquad U_1=B,\qquad U_{\alpha+\gamma}=U_\alpha U_\gamma.
\]
Induction on $m\in\NaturalNumbers$, using $U_{m+1}=U_mB$, then gives
$U_m=B^m$.  Hence the new family at natural order is exactly the usual
natural-power family, as a polynomial identity rather than merely an
identity at selected values of $x$.  In particular this identifies the
two rational polynomial constructions coefficient by coefficient.

Over $\ComplexNumbers$, the function $B$ extends holomorphically across
zero with value $1$.  The nonzero zeros of $\EulerE^t-1$ are
$2\pi k\ImaginaryUnit$, $k\in\IntegerNumbers\setminus\{0\}$, so $B$ is
holomorphic and nonvanishing throughout the disk $|t|<2\pi$.
On this simply connected disk $B'/B$ has a primitive $L$ with $L(0)=0$.
Indeed, integrate $B'/B$ from zero to $t$; Cauchy's theorem makes the
integral independent of the path.  Then
$(\EulerE^{-L}B)'=0$ and $\EulerE^{-L(0)}B(0)=1$, proving
$\EulerE^L=B$.  Any other normalized holomorphic logarithm has the same
derivative $B'/B$, hence equals $L$.
Consequently $\exp(\alpha L(t)+xt)$ is holomorphic on that disk for every
complex $\alpha,x$, and its Taylor series there is
\eqref{eq:merged-norlund-egf}.  This analytic argument is separate from
the formal coefficient identities.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Change of coefficient algebra]
\label{lem:merged-log-base-change}
Let $R,S$ be commutative $\RationalNumbers$-algebras and let
$\varphi:R\to S$ be a unital ring homomorphism.  Write $\varphi_*H$ for
coefficientwise application of $\varphi$ to a formal series $H$.
For every $H\in R[[t]]$ with $H(0)=1$,
\[
 \varphi_*(\log H)=\log(\varphi_*H).
\]
In particular, writing $B_R$ and $B_S$ for the Bernoulli kernels over the
two coefficient algebras,
\[
 \varphi_*B_R=B_S,\qquad
 \varphi_*(\log B_R)=\log B_S.
\]
Thus the rational logarithmic coefficients in
\cref{eq:merged-log-bernoulli-kernel} transfer to every commutative
$\RationalNumbers$-algebra, including $\ComplexNumbers$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
A unital ring homomorphism preserves integer scalars and their inverses
whenever they are units.  It therefore preserves the rational scalars in
these algebras.  Since $H-1$ has constant coefficient zero, each coefficient
of the formal expansion
\[
 \log H=\sum_{m\geq1}\frac{(-1)^{m+1}}m(H-1)^m
\]
involves only finitely many summands: in degree $n$ all $m>n$ vanish.
The homomorphism commutes with those finite sums and products and with
their rational coefficients, proving the first identity.
The coefficients of $B_R$ are the images of $\beta_n/n!$, so their
images under $\varphi$ are precisely the coefficients of $B_S$.
Applying the first identity to $H=B_R$ proves the last assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Appell, difference, and convolution calculus]
\label{thm:merged-norlund-calculus}
For $\alpha,x,y\in R$ and $n\geq1$, with the derivative interpreted
formally in $R[x]$,
\begin{align}
 \frac{\Differential}{\Differential x}\beta_n^{(\alpha)}(x)
 &=n\beta_{n-1}^{(\alpha)}(x),                                    \label{eq:merged-norlund-appell}\\
 \beta_n^{(\alpha)}(x+y)
 &=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom nk\beta_k^{(\alpha)}(x)y^{n-k},           \label{eq:merged-norlund-translation}\\
 \beta_n^{(\alpha)}(x+1)-\beta_n^{(\alpha)}(x)
 &=n\beta_{n-1}^{(\alpha-1)}(x).                                 \label{eq:merged-norlund-difference}
\end{align}
The translation identity also holds at $n=0$.  The difference identity
has the successor-index form
\[
 \beta_{n+1}^{(\alpha+1)}(x+1)-\beta_{n+1}^{(\alpha+1)}(x)
 =(n+1)\beta_n^{(\alpha)}(x)\qquad(n\geq0),
\]
and the difference of the degree-zero polynomial is zero.
More generally, for $\alpha,\gamma,x,y\in R$ and $n\geq0$,
\begin{equation}
 \beta_n^{(\alpha+\gamma)}(x+y)
 =\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom nk
   \beta_k^{(\alpha)}(x)\beta_{n-k}^{(\gamma)}(y).                \label{eq:merged-norlund-convolution}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Differentiating \eqref{eq:merged-norlund-egf} with respect to $x$ multiplies
its left side by $t$.  Replacing $x$ by $x+y$ multiplies it by
$\EulerE^{yt}$.  These observations and the exponential Cauchy product prove
the first two formulas.  The translation identity also holds at $n=0$,
when both sides are $1$.
For the difference identity, the normalized definition gives
$B^\alpha=B^{\alpha-1}B$, since
$\exp((\alpha-1)L)\exp L=\exp(\alpha L)$.
Together with $B(\EulerE^t-1)=t$, this gives
\[
 \left(\frac{t}{\EulerE^t-1}\right)^\alpha
 (\EulerE^{(x+1)t}-\EulerE^{xt})
 =t\left(\frac{t}{\EulerE^t-1}\right)^{\alpha-1}\EulerE^{xt}.
\]
Coefficient comparison proves the third formula, with no restriction on
$\alpha$; at order zero it legitimately uses the order $-1$ family.
Comparing degree $n+1$ after replacing $\alpha$ by $\alpha+1$ gives the
successor-index form, while $\beta_0^{(\alpha)}=1$ proves the zero-degree
case directly.  Multiplication by factorials and their inverses is
performed through rational scalars, so these arguments need no field or
nonzero-ring hypothesis.
Finally $\exp(\alpha L)\exp(\gamma L)=\exp((\alpha+\gamma)L)$, so multiplying
the two generating functions and taking the coefficient of $t^n/n!$
proves the convolution identity, including $n=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal crosswalk]
% ed.: crosswalk added 2026-09-02; natural orders only.
Module \lean{NorlundPolynomials} defines $\beta_n^{(a)}(x)$ for natural
orders $a\in\mathbb N$ by \cref{eq:merged-norlund-egf} with the $a$-th power
of Mathlib's \lean{bernoulliPowerSeries} (\lean{Fabius.norlund}), so that
$\beta_n^{(0)}=x^n$ and $\beta_n^{(1)}=\beta_n$ (\lean{Fabius.norlund_zero},
\lean{Fabius.norlund_one}).  For $a,c\in\mathbb N$ and rational $x,y$:
\cref{eq:merged-norlund-appell} is \lean{Fabius.derivative_norlund_succ},
proved by differentiating the coefficients of $G(t)\EulerE^{xt}$ in $x$
(\lean{Fabius.derivative_coeff_succ_map_C_mul_rescale_exp});
\cref{eq:merged-norlund-convolution} is \lean{Fabius.norlund_add_eval_add},
from the product of the two exponential generating functions
(\lean{Fabius.egfA_norlund_eval}, \lean{Fabius.egfA_mul});
\cref{eq:merged-norlund-translation} is its case $\gamma=0$
(\lean{Fabius.norlund_eval_add}); and \cref{eq:merged-norlund-difference} is
\lean{Fabius.norlund_succ_eval_add_one_sub}, from
$(\EulerE^{(x+1)t}-\EulerE^{xt})\,t/(\EulerE^t-1)=t\EulerE^{xt}$.

The arbitrary-scalar construction is now present as source in
\lean{NorlundGeneralized}, but has not yet passed compiler validation.
Its \lean{Fabius.generalizedNorlund} is an actual polynomial over every
commutative rational algebra.
\lean{Fabius.egfA_generalizedNorlund_eval} states the full EGF,
\lean{Fabius.derivative_generalizedNorlund_succ} the polynomial derivative,
\lean{Fabius.generalizedNorlund_eval_add} the translation, and
\lean{Fabius.generalizedNorlund_add_eval_add} the convolution.
The new difference declarations are
\lean{Fabius.generalizedNorlund_succ_eval_add_one_sub} and
\lean{Fabius.generalizedNorlund_eval_add_one_sub}; the latter uses natural
subtraction on the index and checks degree zero separately.
Neither restricts the scalar order, excludes the zero ring, or assumes
a field.  The natural-order polynomial comparison over
$\RationalNumbers$ is \lean{Fabius.generalizedNorlund_natCast}.
Until this source and its dependencies compile, the row remains
\emph{partial}; no analytic convergence claim follows from the formal EGF.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Bell-polynomial construction and the N\"orlund diagonal]
\label{thm:merged-norlund-bell-diagonal}
Put
\begin{equation}
 c_0(\alpha,x):=0,\qquad c_1(\alpha,x):=x-\frac{\alpha}{2},\qquad
 c_j(\alpha,x):=-\frac{\alpha\beta_j}{j}\quad(j\geq2).            \label{eq:merged-norlund-cumulants}
\end{equation}
Then, for every $n\geq0$,
\begin{align}
 \beta_n^{(\alpha)}(x)
 &=\ExponentialCompleteBellPolynomial n
   \bigl(c_1(\alpha,x),\ldots,c_n(\alpha,x)\bigr)                 \label{eq:merged-norlund-bell}\\
 &=\sum_{\substack{m_1,\ldots,m_n\geq0\\m_1+2m_2+\cdots+nm_n=n}}
 \frac{n!}{\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{n}m_j!\,(j!)^{m_j}}
 \prod_{j=1}^{n}c_j(\alpha,x)^{m_j}.                             \label{eq:merged-norlund-bell-explicit}
\end{align}
At $n=0$, the sum has the unique empty multiplicity vector and its empty
products are $1$; both formulas therefore include $\beta_0^{(\alpha)}(x)=1$.
The diagonal order has the closed form
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle\beta_n^{(n+1)}(x)=\FallingFactorial{x-1}{n}.} \label{eq:merged-norlund-polynomial-diagonal}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for $n\geq1$,
\begin{align}
 \beta_{n-1}^{(n)}(0)&=(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!,                          \label{eq:merged-norlund-number-diagonal}\\
 \CoefficientExtraction t{n-1}
 \left(\frac{t}{\EulerE^t-1}\right)^n&=(-1)^{n-1}.              \label{eq:merged-norlund-diagonal}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $B(t)=t/(\EulerE^t-1)$, with the removable value $B(0)=1$, and
$L(t)=\log B(t)$, normalized by $L(0)=0$.
These expressions may be read as formal series over $R$, with rational
coefficients interpreted through its structure map: the reciprocal kernel
$G(t)=(\EulerE^t-1)/t$ has constant term $1$, and $B(t)G(t)=1$.
Thus the formal logarithm product law also gives $L=-\log G$.
To compute its coefficients without requiring division by $t$,
differentiate $B(\EulerE^t-1)=t$ and multiply by $B$.  The identities
$B(\EulerE^t-1)=t$ and $B\EulerE^t=B+t$ give
\[
 tB'=B-B^2-tB.
\]
Since $BL'=B'$ and $B$ is a unit, cancellation now yields
$tL'=1-B-t$.  With $\beta_1=-1/2$, comparison of coefficients gives
\[
 \frac{\Differential}{\Differential t}\log\frac{t}{\EulerE^t-1}
 =-\frac12-\sum_{j\geq2}\beta_j\frac{t^{j-1}}{j!}.
\]
The logarithm vanishes at $t=0$, so
\begin{equation}
 \log\frac{t}{\EulerE^t-1}
 =-\frac t2-\sum_{j\geq2}\frac{\beta_j}{j}\frac{t^j}{j!}.       \label{eq:merged-log-bernoulli-kernel}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, with $\beta_j^+=\beta_j+\delta_{j1}$, the constant
coefficient of $L$ is zero and every positive-degree coefficient is
$\CoefficientExtraction tj L=-\beta_j^+/(j\,j!)$.
The special value $\beta_1^+=1/2$ explains why the ordinary-Bernoulli
formula in the sum must start at $j=2$.
Thus \eqref{eq:merged-norlund-egf} is
$\exp(\sum_{j\geq1}c_j(\alpha,x)t^j/j!)$.
The complete Bell-polynomial generating function
\cref{thm:bell-poly-egf} proves the first formula.
Apply the generic multiplicity formula
\cref{lem:merged-complete-bell-multiplicities} to this cumulant sequence.
Distributing the powers across $c_j/j!$ and moving the rational
factorials outside the product gives
\cref{eq:merged-norlund-bell-explicit}, including the empty choice at
$n=0$.  Thus the finite-partition expansion is a shared Bell identity,
not a separate property that must be proved again for the N\"orlund family.

For the diagonal, work over the coefficient algebra $R[x]$, with
$(1+z)^{x-1}$ interpreted by its binomial series, and set
$z=\EulerE^t-1$ in a formal residue:
\begin{align*}
 \frac{\beta_n^{(n+1)}(x)}{n!}
 &=\ResidueOperator_t
   \frac{\EulerE^{xt}}{(\EulerE^t-1)^{n+1}}\Differential t
 =\ResidueOperator_z\frac{(1+z)^{x-1}}{z^{n+1}}\Differential z\\
 &=\CoefficientExtraction z n(1+z)^{x-1}=\binom{x-1}{n}.
\end{align*}
The middle equality is \cref{thm:res-subst}.  Multiplication by $n!$ proves
the polynomial diagonal.  Replace $n$ by $n-1$ and set $x=0$ to obtain the
number diagonal; division by $(n-1)!$ gives the coefficient identity.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal crosswalk]
% ed.: crosswalk added 2026-09-02; the Lean proof avoids residues entirely.
Module \lean{NorlundDiagonal} formalizes the three diagonal displays for
natural orders and rational evaluation points:
\cref{eq:merged-norlund-polynomial-diagonal} is
\lean{Fabius.norlund_diagonal}, in the form
$\beta_n^{(n+1)}(x)=\FallingFactorial{x-1}{n}$ with Mathlib's
\lean{descPochhammer}; \cref{eq:merged-norlund-number-diagonal} is
\lean{Fabius.norlund_eval_zero_diagonal}; and
\cref{eq:merged-norlund-diagonal} is
\lean{Fabius.coeff_bernoulliPowerSeries_pow_succ}.
The formal proof does not use \cref{thm:res-subst}, and needs no residue
calculus.  It rests instead on the Riccati equation
$t\,\frac{\Differential}{\Differential t}\!\left(\frac{t}{\EulerE^t-1}\right)
=\frac{t}{\EulerE^t-1}-\left(\frac{t}{\EulerE^t-1}\right)^2-\frac{t^2}{\EulerE^t-1}$
(\lean{Fabius.X_mul_derivative_bernoulliPowerSeries}, obtained by
differentiating $B(\EulerE^t-1)=t$), which converts into the order-lowering
relation
$(a+1)\beta_{n+1}^{(a+2)}(x+1)=(a-n)\beta_{n+1}^{(a+1)}(x)+(n+1)x\beta_n^{(a+1)}(x)$
(\lean{Fabius.norlund_series_step} as generating functions,
\lean{Fabius.norlund_step} coefficientwise).  Taking $a=n$ annihilates the
first term and leaves $\beta_{n+1}^{(n+2)}(x+1)=x\beta_n^{(n+1)}(x)$
(\lean{Fabius.norlund_diagonal_step}); induction on $n$ against
$\FallingFactorial{x-1}{n+1}=(x-1)\FallingFactorial{x-2}{n}$ then gives the
closed form, and $x=0$ with
$\FallingFactorial{-1}{n}=(-1)^nn!$ (\lean{Fabius.descPochhammer_eval_neg_one})
gives the other two.  Transport of the diagonal to an arbitrary coefficient
algebra is not yet a separate proved declaration.

The arbitrary-order Bell construction now has explicit source in
\lean{NorlundGeneralized}:
\lean{Fabius.bellWeightSeries_generalizedNorlundCumulant} identifies the
computed logarithm with the Bell weight series, and
\lean{Fabius.generalizedNorlund_eval_eq_completeBell} states
\cref{eq:merged-norlund-bell}.  The literal finite sum is
\lean{Fabius.generalizedNorlund_eval_eq_sum_weightedPartitions}, with
\lean{Fabius.generalizedNorlund_eval_eq_sum_div_weightedPartitions}
giving its characteristic-zero-field notation.
These specializations use the common formulas of
\cref{lem:merged-complete-bell-multiplicities}.  Their source and the
underlying \lean{BernoulliFormalLog} extension await compiler validation;
the row remains \emph{partial}, without claiming either the general
coefficient-algebra diagonal or analytic convergence.
\end{remark}

\section{Genocchi numbers}

\begin{definition}
The Genocchi numbers $\GenocchiNumber{n}$ are defined by
\begin{equation}
 \frac{2t}{\EulerE^t+1}=\sum_{n\geq1}\GenocchiNumber{n}\frac{t^n}{n!}.                  \label{eq:merged-genocchi-egf}
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Bernoulli--Euler--Genocchi relations]
\label{thm:merged-genocchi}
For $n\geq1$,
\begin{equation}
 \GenocchiNumber{n}=2(1-2^n)\beta_n=n\mathsf E_{n-1}(0).                           \label{eq:merged-genocchi}
\end{equation}
Thus $\GenocchiNumber{1}=1$, $\GenocchiNumber{2m+1}=0$ for $m\geq1$, and
$\GenocchiNumber{2}=-1,\GenocchiNumber{4}=1,\GenocchiNumber{6}=-3,\GenocchiNumber{8}=17,\GenocchiNumber{10}=-155$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use
\[
 \frac{2t}{\EulerE^t+1}=\frac{2t}{\EulerE^t-1}-\frac{4t}{\EulerE^{2t}-1}
\]
and compare Bernoulli coefficients.  Multiplying the Euler EGF at $x=0$ by
$t$ gives the second equality.  Parity and initial values follow immediately.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal crosswalk]
% ed.: crosswalk added 2026-09-01.
Module \lean{GenocchiNumbers} takes the first equality of
\cref{eq:merged-genocchi} as the definition, \lean{Fabius.genocchi}
$=2(1-2^n)\beta_n$, and proves the defining generating function
\cref{eq:merged-genocchi-egf} as \lean{Fabius.egf_genocchi_mul_exp_add_one},
$\bigl(\sum_n\GenocchiNumber{n}t^n/n!\bigr)(\EulerE^t+1)=2t$ over any
$\RationalNumbers$-algebra, by exactly the displayed partial-fraction
identity: the series is $2\mathscr B(t)-2\mathscr B(2t)$
(\lean{Fabius.egf_genocchi_eq}), and Mathlib's
\lean{bernoulliPowerSeries_mul_exp_sub_one} rescaled to $2t$ gives
$(\sum_n\GenocchiNumber{n}t^n/n!)(\EulerE^t+1)(\EulerE^t-1)=2t(\EulerE^t-1)$,
after which $\EulerE^t-1=t\cdot(\text{unit})$ is cancelled.  The parity
statement is \lean{Fabius.genocchi_odd} and the initial values are
\lean{Fabius.genocchi_one} and \lean{Fabius.genocchi_two}; the Euler-polynomial
equality $\GenocchiNumber{n+1}=(n+1)\mathsf E_n(0)$ is \lean{Fabius.genocchi_succ_eq}
(module \lean{EulerPolynomials}), from the two generating functions.
\end{remark}

\section{Bernoulli polynomials of the second kind and Cauchy polynomials}
\label{sec:merged-cauchy-polynomials}

The historical names are not uniform.  The family below is commonly called
the \emph{Bernoulli polynomials of the second kind} and also the
\emph{Cauchy polynomials of the first kind}.  Some supplied manuscripts call
it the Cauchy family ``of the second kind,'' but that phrase is also used for
a distinct reciprocal-log normalization.  We therefore use the neutral
symbol $b_n(x)$ and regard the generating function, rather than the name, as
authoritative; see \cite{Norlund1924}.

\begin{definition}
\label{def:merged-cauchy-polynomials}
Define $b_n(x)$ by
\begin{equation}
 \frac{t(1+t)^x}{\log(1+t)}
 =\sum_{n\geq0}b_n(x)\frac{t^n}{n!}.                               \label{eq:merged-bernoulli-second-egf}
\end{equation}
This is a formal identity with
$(1+t)^x=\sum_{n\geq0}\FallingFactorial{x}{n}t^n/n!$.  Analytically it
holds for $|t|<1$, with the branch determined near $t=0$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Integral, Stirling, difference, addition, and reflection formulas]
\label{thm:merged-cauchy-polynomials}
For every $n\geq0$,
\begin{align}
 b_n(x)&=\int_0^1\FallingFactorial{x+u}{n}\Differential u,          \label{eq:merged-cauchy-integral}\\
 b_n(0)&=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{\SignedStirlingFirstKind{n}{k}}{k+1}. \label{eq:merged-cauchy-stirling-numbers}
\end{align}
For $n\geq1$,
\begin{align}
 b_n'(x)&=n\FallingFactorial{x}{n-1},                              \label{eq:merged-cauchy-derivative}\\
 b_n(x+1)-b_n(x)&=n b_{n-1}(x),                                   \label{eq:merged-cauchy-difference}\\
 b_n(x+y)&=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom nk
 b_k(x)\FallingFactorial{y}{n-k},                                 \label{eq:merged-cauchy-addition}\\
 b_n(x)&=b_n(0)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac nk
 \SignedStirlingFirstKind{n-1}{k-1}x^k.                           \label{eq:merged-cauchy-explicit}
\end{align}
Finally, for every $n\geq0$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle b_n(n-2-x)=(-1)^n b_n(x).}                  \label{eq:merged-cauchy-reflection}
\end{equation}
Thus $b_n$ is even or odd about $(n-2)/2$ according as $n$ is even or odd.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Coefficientwise integration of the falling-factorial binomial series gives
\[
 \sum_{n\geq0}
 \left(\int_0^1\FallingFactorial{x+u}{n}\Differential u\right)
 \frac{t^n}{n!}
 =\int_0^1(1+t)^{x+u}\Differential u
 =(1+t)^x\frac{t}{\log(1+t)}.
\]
This proves the integral representation.  At $x=0$, expand
$\FallingFactorial{u}{n}=\sum_k\SignedStirlingFirstKind{n}{k}u^k$
and integrate to obtain the Stirling formula.

Differentiating the defining EGF with respect to $x$ cancels its logarithm:
\[
 \sum_{n\geq0}b_n'(x)\frac{t^n}{n!}
 =t(1+t)^x.
\]
Coefficient comparison proves the derivative formula.  Replacing $x$ by
$x+1$ and subtracting multiplies the EGF by $t$, proving the difference
formula.  Factoring
\[
 \frac{t(1+t)^{x+y}}{\log(1+t)}
 =\frac{t(1+t)^x}{\log(1+t)}(1+t)^y
\]
proves addition by the exponential Cauchy product.  Expanding
$b_n'(x)=n\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\SignedStirlingFirstKind{n-1}{j}x^j$
and integrating from $0$ to $x$ proves the explicit polynomial formula.

For reflection, use
$\FallingFactorial{n-1-y}{n}=(-1)^n\FallingFactorial{y}{n}$ and put
$v=1-u$ in the integral representation:
\begin{align*}
 b_n(n-2-x)
 &=\int_0^1\FallingFactorial{n-2-x+u}{n}\Differential u
 =\int_0^1\FallingFactorial{n-1-(x+v)}{n}\Differential v\\
 &=(-1)^n\int_0^1\FallingFactorial{x+v}{n}\Differential v
 =(-1)^n b_n(x).
\end{align*}
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal crosswalk]
% ed.: crosswalk added 2026-09-02; the algebraic identities are formal, the integral ones are not.
Module \lean{CauchyPolynomials} defines $b_n(x)$ directly by
\cref{eq:merged-bernoulli-second-egf}, as $n!$ times the $n$-th coefficient of
$t(1+t)^x/\log(1+t)$ in $\mathbb Q[x][[t]]$ (\lean{Fabius.cauchyPoly}), the
factor $t/\log(1+t)$ being the power-series inverse of the Gregory series
\lean{Fabius.logDivSeries}.  The supporting object is the falling-factorial
generating function $(1+t)^u=\sum_n\FallingFactorial un t^n/n!$ over any
$\mathbb Q$-algebra (\lean{Fabius.fallingSeries}, module
\lean{FallingFactorialSeries}), characterised by $(1+t)F'=uF$ with $F(0)=1$;
from that characterisation one gets the product law
$(1+t)^u(1+t)^v=(1+t)^{u+v}$ (\lean{Fabius.fallingSeries_mul}), hence the
Vandermonde identity for falling factorials
(\lean{Fabius.descPochhammer_eval_add}), and, in $\mathbb Q[x][[t]]$, the
$x$-derivative $\partial_x(1+t)^x=\log(1+t)\,(1+t)^x$
(\lean{Fabius.Dx_fallingPoly}, with $\partial_x$ the coefficientwise
derivative \lean{Fabius.Dx}).
Consequently \cref{eq:merged-cauchy-derivative} is
\lean{Fabius.derivative_cauchyPoly_succ}, \cref{eq:merged-cauchy-difference}
is \lean{Fabius.cauchyPoly_succ_eval_add_one_sub},
\cref{eq:merged-cauchy-addition} is \lean{Fabius.cauchyPoly_eval_add} and
\cref{eq:merged-cauchy-explicit} is \lean{Fabius.cauchyPoly_succ_eq}.
\cref{eq:merged-cauchy-stirling-numbers} is \lean{Fabius.cauchyPoly_eval_zero},
proved with the formal integral $\int_0^1p(u)\Differential u=\sum_kp_k/(k+1)$
as a linear functional on $\mathbb Q[u]$ (\lean{Fabius.intPoly}), for which
$\int_0^1p'=p(1)-p(0)$ (\lean{Fabius.intPoly_derivative}); applying it
coefficientwise to $\partial_x(1+t)^x$ gives
$\int_0^1(1+t)^u\Differential u=t/\log(1+t)$
(\lean{Fabius.mapIntPoly_fallingPoly}), which is
\cref{eq:merged-cauchy-integral} in formal form, although the analytic
integral representation itself is not formalized.  The reflection
\cref{eq:merged-cauchy-reflection} is not formalized.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
The first polynomials, useful for checking the normalization, are
\[
 b_0(x)=1,\quad b_1(x)=x+\frac12,\quad b_2(x)=x^2-\frac16,
\]
\[
 b_3(x)=x^3-\frac32x^2+\frac14,\qquad
 b_4(x)=x^4-4x^3+4x^2-\frac{19}{30}.
\]
In particular $b_n(0)=1,1/2,-1/6,1/4,-19/30,\ldots$, which distinguishes
this normalization from the other Cauchy families.
\end{remark}

\section{A modified Bernoulli normalization}

Define the even ordinary-series coefficients $\beta_{2m}^{\sharp}$ by
\begin{equation}
 \frac12\log\!\left(\frac{\EulerE^{x/2}-\EulerE^{-x/2}}{x/2}\right)
 =\sum_{m\geq0}\beta_{2m}^{\sharp}x^{2m},
 \qquad \beta_0^{\sharp}=\frac12\log2.                              \label{eq:merged-modified-bernoulli-def}
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}
For even $n\geq2$,
\begin{equation}
 \beta_n^{\sharp}=\frac{\beta_n}{2n^2\GammaFunction(n)},                    \label{eq:merged-modified-bernoulli}
\end{equation}
and odd coefficients vanish.  In particular,
$\beta_2^{\sharp}=1/48$, $\beta_4^{\sharp}=-1/5760$, and
$\beta_6^{\sharp}=1/362880$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate the defining series:
\[
 \frac14\coth\frac x2-\frac1{2x}
 =\frac1{2x}\left(\frac x2\coth\frac x2-1\right).
\]
The Bernoulli generating function implies
\[
 \frac x2\coth\frac x2=\sum_{m\geq0}\beta_{2m}\frac{x^{2m}}{(2m)!}.
\]
Integrating termwise gives
$\beta_{2m}^{\sharp}=\beta_{2m}/(4m(2m)!)$, which is the displayed formula.
\end{proof}

\section{Periodic Bernoulli functions and Fourier expansion}
\label{sec:merged-periodic-bernoulli}

The value of the first periodic Bernoulli function at its jumps must be
chosen separately: a Fourier series converges at a jump to the midpoint of
the two one-sided limits.

\begin{definition}[Periodic Bernoulli functions]
\label{def:merged-periodic-bernoulli}
For $n\geq2$, put $\widetilde\beta_n(x):=\beta_n(\{x\})$.  For $n=1$, put
\begin{equation}
 \widetilde\beta_1(x):=
 \begin{cases}
  \{x\}-\dfrac12,&x\notin\IntegerNumbers,\\[1mm]
  0,&x\in\IntegerNumbers.
 \end{cases}                                                       \label{eq:merged-periodic-bernoulli-one-def}
\end{equation}
Thus $\widetilde\beta_n$ is continuous and $1$-periodic for $n\geq2$,
whereas $\widetilde\beta_1$ is the midpoint-normalized periodic sawtooth.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Fourier expansion with exact convergence qualifications]
\label{thm:merged-periodic-bernoulli-fourier}
For $n\geq2$ and every real $x$,
\begin{equation}
 \widetilde\beta_n(x)
 =-n!\sum_{k\in\IntegerNumbers\setminus\{0\}}
 \frac{\EulerE^{2\pi \ImaginaryUnit kx}}{(2\pi \ImaginaryUnit k)^n}.                          \label{eq:merged-periodic-bernoulli-fourier-complex}
\end{equation}
The series converges absolutely and uniformly.  In degree one,
\begin{equation}
 \widetilde\beta_1(x)
 =-\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{0<|k|\leq N}
 \frac{\EulerE^{2\pi \ImaginaryUnit kx}}{2\pi \ImaginaryUnit k}
 =-\frac1\pi\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sin(2\pi kx)}{k}.         \label{eq:merged-periodic-bernoulli-fourier-one}
\end{equation}
The symmetric complex series and the paired sine series converge at every
real $x$, and their value at an integer is $0$.  The complex series is nowhere
absolutely convergent, and its symmetric partial sums do not converge
uniformly.  (The paired sine series is trivially zero at half-integers, so it
would be incorrect to call that particular scalar series conditional there.)

Pairing positive and negative frequencies gives, for $m\geq1$,
\begin{align}
 \widetilde\beta_{2m}(x)
 &=(-1)^{m+1}\frac{2(2m)!}{(2\pi)^{2m}}
 \sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\cos(2\pi kx)}{k^{2m}},                 \label{eq:merged-periodic-bernoulli-fourier-even}\\
 \widetilde\beta_{2m+1}(x)
 &=(-1)^{m+1}\frac{2(2m+1)!}{(2\pi)^{2m+1}}
 \sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sin(2\pi kx)}{k^{2m+1}}.             \label{eq:merged-periodic-bernoulli-fourier-odd}
\end{align}
In particular,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \RiemannZeta(2m)=(-1)^{m+1}
 \frac{(2\pi)^{2m}}{2(2m)!}\,\beta_{2m}.}                        \label{eq:merged-even-zeta-bernoulli}
\end{equation}
For $n\geq2$,
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{x\in\RealNumbers}|\widetilde\beta_n(x)|
 \leq\frac{2n!\RiemannZeta(n)}{(2\pi)^n}.                        \label{eq:merged-periodic-bernoulli-sup}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $0<r<1$ and $0<x<1$, the logarithm series gives
\[
 \sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{r^k\EulerE^{2\pi \ImaginaryUnit kx}}k
 =-\log(1-r\EulerE^{2\pi \ImaginaryUnit x}).
\]
Taking imaginary parts and then letting $r\uparrow1$ yields
\[
 \sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sin(2\pi kx)}k
 =\pi\left(\frac12-x\right),
\]
because $\arg(1-\EulerE^{2\pi \ImaginaryUnit x})=\pi x-\pi/2$.  Dirichlet's test gives
the same ordinary-series limit: $1/k$ decreases to zero and the sine partial
sums are bounded for fixed nonintegral $x$.  At an integer every sine term is
zero.  The complex series is nowhere absolutely convergent because the sum of
the moduli of its terms is a constant multiple of $\sum_{k\geq1}1/k$.  Its
symmetric partial sums are continuous, whereas their pointwise limit has
jumps at the integers; hence convergence cannot be uniform.  This proves the
degree-one statement and its convergence claims.

For $n\geq1$, let
\[
 c_k^{(n)}=\int_0^1\beta_n(x)\EulerE^{-2\pi \ImaginaryUnit kx}\Differential x.
\]
The mean coefficient is zero, because the Appell identity gives
\[
 \int_0^1\beta_n(x)\Differential x
 =\frac{\beta_{n+1}(1)-\beta_{n+1}(0)}{n+1}=0.
\]
For $k\ne0$ and $n\geq2$, the endpoint values agree, so integration by parts
and $\beta_n'=n\beta_{n-1}$ give
$c_k^{(n)}=n c_k^{(n-1)}/(2\pi \ImaginaryUnit k)$.  Direct integration gives
$c_k^{(1)}=-1/(2\pi \ImaginaryUnit k)$; hence
\begin{equation}
 c_k^{(n)}=-\frac{n!}{(2\pi \ImaginaryUnit k)^n}\qquad(k\ne0).                  \label{eq:merged-periodic-bernoulli-coefficients}
\end{equation}

For $n\geq2$, the claimed series is absolutely and uniformly convergent by
comparison with $2n!\sum_{k\geq1}k^{-n}/(2\pi)^n$.  It defines a continuous
$1$-periodic function with exactly the coefficients just computed.  To make
the reconstruction step explicit, let $h$ be its difference from
$\widetilde\beta_n$.  Every Fourier coefficient of $h$ is zero, so every
Fejér mean is zero:
\[
 \sigma_Nh(x)=\sum_{|k|\leq N}
 \left(1-\frac{|k|}{N+1}\right)\FourierTwoPi{h}(k)\EulerE^{2\pi \ImaginaryUnit kx}=0.
\]
But $\sigma_Nh=h*K_N$, where
\[
 K_N(y)=\frac1{N+1}
 \left(\frac{\sin\pi(N+1)y}{\sin\pi y}\right)^2.
\]
The Fejér kernels are nonnegative, have integral one, and concentrate at
zero.  Uniform continuity of $h$ therefore gives $\sigma_Nh\to h$ uniformly,
so $h=0$.

Pairing $k$ and $-k$ proves the sine and cosine formulas.  Put $x=0$ in the
even formula to obtain the zeta identity.  Taking absolute values term by
term in the complex formula proves the supremum bound.
\end{proof}

\section{Euler--Maclaurin summation}

Put
\[
 P_r(x):=\frac{\widetilde\beta_r(x)}{r!}.
\]
The value assigned to $P_1$ at an integer does not affect the integrals below.

\begin{theorem}[Euler--Maclaurin with remainder]
\label{thm:merged-euler-maclaurin}
Let $m\geq1$ be an integer, let $a<b$ be integers, and let
$f\in C^{2m}([a,b])$.  Then
\begin{align}
 \sum_{k=a}^{b}f(k)
 ={}&\int_a^b f(x)\Differential x+\frac{f(a)+f(b)}2                           \notag\\
 &+\sum_{r=1}^{m}\frac{\beta_{2r}}{(2r)!}
 \bigl(f^{(2r-1)}(b)-f^{(2r-1)}(a)\bigr)+R_m,                      \label{eq:merged-euler-maclaurin}
\end{align}
where
\begin{equation}
 R_m=-\frac1{(2m)!}\int_a^b\beta_{2m}(\{x\})f^{(2m)}(x)\Differential x       \label{eq:merged-euler-maclaurin-rem}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
 |R_m|\leq\frac{2\RiemannZeta(2m)}{(2\pi)^{2m}}
                  \int_a^b|f^{(2m)}(x)|\Differential x.                     \label{eq:merged-euler-maclaurin-bound}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
On $[k,k+1]$, integration by parts gives
\[
 \int_k^{k+1}\left(x-k-\frac12\right)f'(x)\Differential x
 =\frac{f(k)+f(k+1)}2-\int_k^{k+1}f(x)\Differential x.
\]
Summing yields the trapezoidal term plus $\int_a^bP_1f'$.  Integrate by parts
repeatedly, using $P_r'=P_{r-1}$ away from integers and continuity of $P_r$ at
integers for $r\geq2$.  Boundary values are $\beta_r/r!$; odd Bernoulli
numbers beyond $\beta_1$ vanish.  The surviving boundary terms and final
integral are exactly \eqref{eq:merged-euler-maclaurin} and
\eqref{eq:merged-euler-maclaurin-rem}.

By \eqref{eq:merged-periodic-bernoulli-sup},
\[
 \sup_{x\in\RealNumbers}|P_{2m}(x)|
 \leq\frac{2\RiemannZeta(2m)}{(2\pi)^{2m}}.
\]
Substitution in \eqref{eq:merged-euler-maclaurin-rem} proves
\eqref{eq:merged-euler-maclaurin-bound}.
\end{proof}

Both asymptotic applications below --- Stirling's expansion and the
harmonic-number expansion --- need the same manoeuvre: the lower endpoint
contributes nothing that depends on $N$, and its contribution has to be
collected into a single constant that is then identified by a limit.  It is
worth isolating that step once.

\begin{corollary}[Constant-of-integration form]
\label{cor:merged-euler-maclaurin-constant}
Let $m\ge1$, let $a$ be an integer, and let $f\in C^{2m}([a,\infty))$ have
$f^{(2m)}$ absolutely integrable on $[a,\infty)$.  Then there is a constant
$C=C(f,a,m)$, independent of $N$, such that for every integer $N>a$
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=a}^{N}f(k)=\int_a^Nf(x)\Differential x+\frac{f(N)}2+C
 +\sum_{r=1}^{m}\frac{\beta_{2r}}{(2r)!}f^{(2r-1)}(N)+\rho_m(N),  \label{eq:merged-euler-maclaurin-constant}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
 \rho_m(N)=\frac1{(2m)!}\int_N^\infty\beta_{2m}(\{x\})f^{(2m)}(x)\Differential x,
 \qquad
 |\rho_m(N)|\le\frac{2\RiemannZeta(2m)}{(2\pi)^{2m}(2m)!}
 \int_N^\infty|f^{(2m)}(x)|\Differential x.                       \label{eq:merged-euler-maclaurin-constant-rem}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Take $b=N$ in \eqref{eq:merged-euler-maclaurin} and split the remainder
integral as $\int_a^N=\int_a^\infty-\int_N^\infty$, which is legitimate because
$\beta_{2m}(\{\cdot\})$ is bounded and $f^{(2m)}$ is absolutely integrable.
Every term that involves only $a$ --- namely $f(a)/2$, the boundary sum
$-\sum_r\beta_{2r}f^{(2r-1)}(a)/(2r)!$, and
$-\int_a^\infty\beta_{2m}(\{x\})f^{(2m)}(x)\Differential x/(2m)!$ --- is
independent of $N$, and their sum is the constant $C$.  What survives is
\eqref{eq:merged-euler-maclaurin-constant}, and the bound on $\rho_m$ is
\eqref{eq:merged-periodic-bernoulli-sup} applied to the tail.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Stirling's expansion]
For each fixed integer $M\geq1$, as $n\to\infty$ through positive integers,
\begin{equation}
 \log(n!)=\left(n+\frac12\right)\log n-n+\frac12\log(2\pi)
 +\sum_{r=1}^{M-1}\frac{\beta_{2r}}{2r(2r-1)n^{2r-1}}
 +\BigO(n^{-(2M-1)}).                                                    \label{eq:merged-stirling-em}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{cor:merged-euler-maclaurin-constant} to $f(x)=\log x$ with $a=1$;
the hypothesis holds because $|f^{(2M)}(x)|=(2M-1)!\,x^{-2M}$ is integrable on
$[1,\infty)$.  Here $\int_1^N\log x\Differential x=N\log N-N+1$ and
$f(N)/2=\frac12\log N$, while
$f^{(2r-1)}(x)=(2r-2)!\,x^{-(2r-1)}$ gives the displayed inverse powers
$\beta_{2r}/\bigl(2r(2r-1)N^{2r-1}\bigr)$.  The constant $C$ supplied by the
corollary is evaluated as $\frac12\log(2\pi)-1$ by Wallis' product, and the
remainder bound one order farther gives the stated error.
\end{proof}

The same machine applied to $f(x)=1/x$ produces the companion expansion for the
harmonic numbers, and identifies the Euler--Mascheroni constant as the
associated constant of integration.  This is the expansion used silently in
several coefficient estimates elsewhere in this work.

\begin{corollary}[Harmonic-number expansion]\label{cor:merged-harmonic-expansion}
Let $H_N=\sum_{k=1}^N1/k$.  For each fixed integer $M\ge1$, as $N\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}
 H_N=\log N+\gamma+\frac1{2N}
 -\sum_{r=1}^{M-1}\frac{\beta_{2r}}{2r\,N^{2r}}+\BigO(N^{-2M}),   \label{eq:merged-harmonic-expansion}
\end{equation}
so that explicitly
$H_N=\log N+\gamma+\frac1{2N}-\frac1{12N^2}+\frac1{120N^4}+\BigO(N^{-6})$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{cor:merged-euler-maclaurin-constant} with $m=M$, $f(x)=1/x$ and
$a=1$; the hypothesis holds because $|f^{(2M)}(x)|=(2M)!\,x^{-2M-1}$ is
integrable on $[1,\infty)$.  Here $\int_1^N f=\log N$, $f(N)/2=1/(2N)$, and
$f^{(2r-1)}(x)=-(2r-1)!\,x^{-2r}$, so
$\beta_{2r}f^{(2r-1)}(N)/(2r)!=-\beta_{2r}/(2r\,N^{2r})$.  Hence
\begin{equation}
 H_N=\log N+\frac1{2N}+C
 -\sum_{r=1}^{M}\frac{\beta_{2r}}{2r\,N^{2r}}+\rho_M(N).           \label{eq:merged-harmonic-em}
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:merged-euler-maclaurin-constant-rem},
\[
 |\rho_M(N)|\le\frac{2\RiemannZeta(2M)}{(2\pi)^{2M}(2M)!}
 \int_N^\infty(2M)!\,x^{-2M-1}\Differential x
 =\frac{2\RiemannZeta(2M)}{(2M)(2\pi)^{2M}}\,N^{-2M}=\BigO(N^{-2M}),
\]
so every term of \eqref{eq:merged-harmonic-em} except $C$ tends to $0$ or is
accounted for, and letting $N\to\infty$ gives
$C=\lim_N(H_N-\log N)=\gamma$ --- for every $M$ at once, since $C$ is the only
$N$-independent term.  Finally the term $r=M$ of the sum is itself
$\BigO(N^{-2M})$ and may be absorbed into the error, which leaves
\eqref{eq:merged-harmonic-expansion}.  The displayed special case uses
$\beta_2=1/6$ and $\beta_4=-1/30$.
\end{proof}

\chapter{Pochhammer symbols, polygamma functions, and Barnes' \texorpdfstring{$G$}{G}-function}
\label{ch:merged-gamma-barnes}

\section{Pochhammer algebra}

\begin{definition}
The rising Pochhammer symbol is
\begin{equation}
 (a)_n=\RisingFactorial an=a(a+1)\cdots(a+n-1),\qquad(a)_0=1.                \label{eq:merged-pochhammer}
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Gamma quotient and binomial series]
\label{thm:merged-pochhammer}
Where defined meromorphically,
\begin{equation}
 (a)_n=\frac{\GammaFunction(a+n)}{\GammaFunction(a)},                               \label{eq:merged-pochhammer-gamma}
\end{equation}
and, formally in $z$ and analytically for $|z|<1$,
\begin{equation}
 (1-z)^{-a}=\sum_{n\geq0}\frac{(a)_n}{n!}z^n.                      \label{eq:merged-binomial-series}
\end{equation}
Moreover, for all $m,n\in\NaturalNumbers$,
\begin{equation}
 (a)_{m+n}=(a)_m\,(a+m)_n,\qquad
 \FallingFactorial{a}{m+n}=\FallingFactorial{a}{m}\FallingFactorial{a-m}{n}. \label{eq:merged-pochhammer-split}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Iterate $\GammaFunction(w+1)=w\GammaFunction(w)$ to obtain the quotient.  For the series, let
$F=\sum(a)_nz^n/n!$.  The recurrence $(a)_{n+1}=(a+n)(a)_n$ gives
$(1-z)F'=aF$ and $F(0)=1$, whose unique formal solution is $(1-z)^{-a}$.
The ratio test gives analytic convergence in the unit disk.  The splitting
rules \eqref{eq:merged-pochhammer-split} are immediate from the definition:
the product $a(a+1)\cdots(a+m+n-1)$ breaks after its $m$th factor, and the
falling statement is the same identity read in the opposite direction.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Chu--Vandermonde]
\begin{equation}
 \frac{(a+b)_n}{n!}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(a)_k}{k!}
                         \frac{(b)_{n-k}}{(n-k)!}.                  \label{eq:merged-vandermonde-rising}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Multiply the binomial series for exponents $a$ and $b$ and compare with the
series for exponent $a+b$.
\end{proof}

For $r\geq1$, define shifted generalized harmonic sums
\begin{equation}
 H_n^{(r)}(a)=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}(a+j)^{-r},\qquad H_n(a)=H_n^{(1)}(a). \label{eq:merged-shifted-harmonic}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[All parameter derivatives]
\label{thm:merged-pochhammer-derivatives}
For $m\geq1$ away from the apparent poles,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\partial^m}{\partial a^m}(a)_n
 =(a)_n\ExponentialCompleteBellPolynomial m\!\left(H_n(a),-1!H_n^{(2)}(a),2!H_n^{(3)}(a),\ldots,
 (-1)^{m-1}(m-1)!H_n^{(m)}(a)\right).                              \label{eq:merged-pochhammer-derivatives}
\end{equation}
For $m=0$ the corresponding statement is the identity
$(a)_n=(a)_n\ExponentialCompleteBellPolynomial0$, with $\ExponentialCompleteBellPolynomial0=1$.  The right side extends
polynomially across all removable singularities.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $L(a)=\log(a)_n$.  Then
$L^{(r)}(a)=(-1)^{r-1}(r-1)!H_n^{(r)}(a)$.  Apply the exponential case of
Faà di Bruno to $\EulerE^{L(a)}$.  Since the left side is a polynomial in $a$,
agreement off the zeros supplies the polynomial continuation.
\end{proof}

The reciprocal needs no separate computation.  The complete Bell polynomial
$\ExponentialCompleteBellPolynomial m$ is a sum of monomials
$x_1^{j_1}x_2^{j_2}\cdots$ with $\sum_i ij_i=m$, so substituting
$x_i\mapsto(-1)^ix_i$ multiplies every monomial by $(-1)^{\sum_iij_i}=(-1)^m$:
\begin{equation}
 \ExponentialCompleteBellPolynomial m(-x_1,x_2,-x_3,\ldots,(-1)^mx_m)
 =(-1)^m\ExponentialCompleteBellPolynomial m(x_1,x_2,x_3,\ldots,x_m).      \label{eq:merged-bell-sign-flip}
\end{equation}

\begin{corollary}[Derivatives of a reciprocal Pochhammer symbol]
\label{cor:merged-reciprocal-pochhammer}
For $m\ge1$ and $(a)_n\ne0$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\partial^m}{\partial a^m}\frac1{(a)_n}
 =\frac{(-1)^m}{(a)_n}
 \ExponentialCompleteBellPolynomial m\!\left(H_n(a),-1!H_n^{(2)}(a),2!H_n^{(3)}(a),\ldots,
 (-1)^{m-1}(m-1)!H_n^{(m)}(a)\right),                              \label{eq:merged-reciprocal-pochhammer}
\end{equation}
with exactly the arguments of \eqref{eq:merged-pochhammer-derivatives}.  In
particular the two derivative arrays differ only by the alternating sign
$(-1)^m$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
With $L(a)=\log(a)_n$ as above, $1/(a)_n=\EulerE^{-L(a)}$, and the exponential
Faà di Bruno formula gives
\[
 \frac{\partial^m}{\partial a^m}\EulerE^{-L(a)}
 =\EulerE^{-L(a)}\ExponentialCompleteBellPolynomial m\bigl(-L'(a),-L''(a),\ldots,-L^{(m)}(a)\bigr).
\]
Since $L^{(r)}(a)=(-1)^{r-1}(r-1)!H_n^{(r)}(a)$, the $r$th argument is
$-L^{(r)}(a)=(-1)^r(r-1)!H_n^{(r)}(a)$, which is $(-1)^r$ times the $r$th
argument in \eqref{eq:merged-pochhammer-derivatives}.  Now apply
\eqref{eq:merged-bell-sign-flip}.
\end{proof}

\section{Euler's limit and the Weierstrass product}

The partial-fraction series of the next section are obtained by
differentiating Weierstrass' product for $1/\GammaFunction$.  That product is
proved here, so that the chapter rests on nothing beyond the integral
definition of $\GammaFunction$.  The one ingredient is the Beta integral with
an integer parameter, which is also the exact statement that converts a
Pochhammer symbol into an integral.

\begin{proposition}[Beta integral with an integer parameter]
\label{prop:merged-beta-integral}
For $\Re a>0$ and every integer $n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \int_0^1t^{a-1}(1-t)^n\Differential t=\frac{n!}{(a)_{n+1}}.       \label{eq:merged-beta-integral}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Induct on $n$.  For $n=0$ the integral is $1/a=0!/(a)_1$.  For $n\ge1$,
integration by parts with $u=(1-t)^n$ and $\Differential v=t^{a-1}\Differential t$
gives, the boundary terms vanishing because $\Re a>0$ and $n\ge1$,
\[
 \int_0^1t^{a-1}(1-t)^n\Differential t
 =\frac na\int_0^1t^{a}(1-t)^{n-1}\Differential t.
\]
The inductive hypothesis at parameter $a+1$ turns the right side into
$\frac na\cdot\frac{(n-1)!}{(a+1)_n}=\frac{n!}{a\,(a+1)_n}=\frac{n!}{(a)_{n+1}}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Euler's limit and the Weierstrass product]
\label{thm:merged-euler-gamma-limit}
For $\Re z>0$,
\begin{equation}
 \GammaFunction(z)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{N!\,N^z}{z(z+1)\cdots(z+N)},   \label{eq:merged-euler-gamma-limit}
\end{equation}
locally uniformly on $\{\Re z>0\}$.  Moreover, for every $z\in\ComplexNumbers$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac1{\GammaFunction(z)}=z\EulerE^{\gamma z}
 \prod_{k\ge1}\left(1+\frac zk\right)\EulerE^{-z/k},                \label{eq:merged-weierstrass-gamma}
\end{equation}
the product converging locally uniformly, so that $1/\GammaFunction$ is entire
with simple zeros exactly at $0,-1,-2,\ldots$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put $\GammaFunction_N(z)=\int_0^N\bigl(1-\tfrac tN\bigr)^Nt^{z-1}\Differential t$.
Substituting $t=Nu$ and applying \cref{prop:merged-beta-integral} gives
\begin{equation}
 \GammaFunction_N(z)=N^z\int_0^1(1-u)^Nu^{z-1}\Differential u
 =\frac{N^z\,N!}{(z)_{N+1}}
 =\frac{N!\,N^z}{z(z+1)\cdots(z+N)}.                               \label{eq:merged-euler-gamma-finite}
\end{equation}
For $0\le t\le N$ the elementary inequality
$0\le(1-t/N)^N\le\EulerE^{-t}$ holds, and $(1-t/N)^N\to\EulerE^{-t}$
pointwise.  On a compact subset of $\{\Re z>0\}$ one has
$\delta\le\Re z\le M$ for some $0<\delta\le M$, whence
$|t^{z-1}|=t^{\Re z-1}\le t^{\delta-1}+t^{M-1}$, an integrable majorant
against $\EulerE^{-t}$ on $(0,\infty)$.  Dominated convergence therefore gives
$\GammaFunction_N(z)\to\int_0^\infty\EulerE^{-t}t^{z-1}\Differential t=\GammaFunction(z)$
for each fixed $z$ with $\Re z>0$.  The convergence is in fact locally uniform,
but that does not follow from dominated convergence alone: each
$\GammaFunction_N$ is holomorphic on $\{\Re z>0\}$, and the same majorant bounds
the whole family uniformly on each compact subset, so Vitali's theorem upgrades
pointwise convergence of a locally bounded holomorphic family to local uniform
convergence.  This is
\eqref{eq:merged-euler-gamma-limit}.

For the product, invert \eqref{eq:merged-euler-gamma-finite}:
\[
 \frac1{\GammaFunction_N(z)}
 =z\,\EulerE^{-z\log N}\prod_{k=1}^{N}\left(1+\frac zk\right)
 =z\,\EulerE^{\gamma_Nz}\prod_{k=1}^{N}\left(1+\frac zk\right)\EulerE^{-z/k},
\]
where $\gamma_N=\sum_{k=1}^N\frac1k-\log N$, since the inserted factors
$\EulerE^{-z/k}$ contribute exactly $\EulerE^{-z\sum_{k\le N}1/k}$.  Now
$\gamma_N\to\gamma$, and the product converges locally uniformly on
$\ComplexNumbers$: for $|w|\le\frac12$ one has
$\bigl|\log\bigl((1+w)\EulerE^{-w}\bigr)\bigr|\le|w|^2$, so on $|z|\le\rho$ all
factors with $k\ge2\rho$ satisfy
$|\log((1+z/k)\EulerE^{-z/k})|\le\rho^2/k^2$, a convergent bound.  Letting
$N\to\infty$ gives \eqref{eq:merged-weierstrass-gamma} on $\{\Re z>0\}$, and
both sides are entire, so the identity persists on $\ComplexNumbers$ by
analytic continuation.  The zeros of the right side are visibly simple and
located at $0,-1,-2,\ldots$.
\end{proof}

\section{Digamma and polygamma series}

Define $\psi=\GammaFunction'/\GammaFunction$ and $\psi^{(m)}=D^m\psi$.

\begin{theorem}[Partial fractions]
\label{thm:merged-polygamma-series}
Away from nonpositive integers,
\begin{align}
 \psi(z)&=-\gamma+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}
     \left(\frac1{k+1}-\frac1{k+z}\right),                          \label{eq:merged-digamma-series}\\
 \psi^{(m)}(z)&=(-1)^{m+1}m!\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+z)^{-m-1}
 \quad(m\geq1).                                                     \label{eq:merged-polygamma-series}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Logarithmically differentiate the Weierstrass product
\eqref{eq:merged-weierstrass-gamma}, which
\cref{thm:merged-euler-gamma-limit} establishes:
\[
 -\psi(z)=\frac1z+\gamma
 +\sum_{k\ge1}\left(\frac1{k+z}-\frac1k\right).
\]
Combining the term $1/z$ with the sum, and pairing $1/k$ with the index shift,
gives \eqref{eq:merged-digamma-series}.  The local uniform convergence proved
in \cref{thm:merged-euler-gamma-limit} is normal on compact sets away from the
poles, so the series may be differentiated termwise any number of times, which
gives \eqref{eq:merged-polygamma-series}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Recurrences and special values]
\begin{align}
 \psi(z+1)&=\psi(z)+z^{-1},                                         \label{eq:merged-digamma-rec}\\
 \psi^{(m)}(z+1)&=\psi^{(m)}(z)+(-1)^mm!z^{-m-1},                   \label{eq:merged-polygamma-rec}\\
 \psi(n)&=H_{n-1}-\gamma,                                           \label{eq:merged-digamma-int}\\
 \psi^{(m)}(1)&=(-1)^{m+1}m!\RiemannZeta(m+1).                     \label{eq:merged-polygamma-one}
\end{align}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Subtract the partial-fraction series at consecutive arguments; the series
telescopes.  Iterate from $\psi(1)=-\gamma$, and set $z=1$ in the polygamma
series.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Hurwitz-zeta form]\label{cor:merged-polygamma-hurwitz}
For $m\geq1$ and $\Re z>0$,
\begin{equation}
 \psi^{(m)}(z)=(-1)^{m+1}m!\,\HurwitzZeta{m+1}{z}.                \label{eq:merged-polygamma-hurwitz}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The Hurwitz zeta function is $\HurwitzZeta sz=\sum_{k\geq0}(k+z)^{-s}$ for
$\Re s>1$ and $\Re z>0$, which is precisely the series in
\eqref{eq:merged-polygamma-series} at $s=m+1$.
\end{proof}

This is the bridge to the Stirling--Hurwitz sums of
\cref{thm:stirling-zeta}: the same Hurwitz values occur there as the limits of
first-kind Stirling series, and \eqref{eq:merged-polygamma-hurwitz} identifies
them as polygamma values.

\begin{theorem}[Reflection]
\label{thm:merged-polygamma-reflection}
For $z\notin\IntegerNumbers$,
\begin{align}
 \psi(1-z)-\psi(z)&=\pi\cot(\pi z),                                 \label{eq:merged-digamma-reflection}\\
 (-1)^m\psi^{(m)}(1-z)-\psi^{(m)}(z)
 &=\pi\frac{\Differential^m}{\Differential z^m}\cot(\pi z).                            \label{eq:merged-polygamma-reflection}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Multiplying the Weierstrass products for $1/\GammaFunction(1+z)$ and
$1/\GammaFunction(1-z)$ and using Euler's sine product gives
$\GammaFunction(z)\GammaFunction(1-z)=\pi/\sin(\pi z)$.  Logarithmic differentiation proves
the first formula; differentiating it $m$ more times proves the second.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Gauss multiplication]
\label{thm:merged-gauss-multiplication}
For $q\in\PositiveIntegers$,
\begin{equation}
 \GammaFunction(qz)=(2\pi)^{(1-q)/2}q^{qz-1/2}
 \prod_{r=0}^{q-1}\GammaFunction\!\left(z+\frac rq\right).                 \label{eq:merged-gauss-multiplication}
\end{equation}
Hence
\begin{align}
 \sum_{r=0}^{q-1}\psi\!\left(z+\frac rq\right)
 &=q\psi(qz)-q\log q,                                              \label{eq:merged-digamma-mult}\\
 \sum_{r=0}^{q-1}\psi^{(m)}\!\left(z+\frac rq\right)
 &=q^{m+1}\psi^{(m)}(qz).                                          \label{eq:merged-polygamma-mult}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Define
\[
 Q(z)=\frac{q^{qz-1/2}\prod_{r=0}^{q-1}\GammaFunction(z+r/q)}{\GammaFunction(qz)}.
\]
The gamma recurrence shows $Q(z+1/q)=Q(z)$.  Stirling's formula in a right
half-plane gives $Q(z)\to(2\pi)^{(q-1)/2}$ as $\Re z\to+\infty$ in a fixed
horizontal strip.  Periodicity therefore makes $Q$ equal to that constant;
meromorphic continuation extends the identity throughout its domain.  Solving
for $\GammaFunction(qz)$ proves \eqref{eq:merged-gauss-multiplication}.  Logarithmic
differentiation gives the digamma identity and further differentiation gives
the polygamma identity.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
For every $m\geq0$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\partial^{m+1}}{\partial a^{m+1}}\log(a)_n
 =\psi^{(m)}(a+n)-\psi^{(m)}(a)
 =(-1)^m m!H_n^{(m+1)}(a).                                        \label{eq:merged-pochhammer-polygamma}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Logarithmically differentiate the gamma quotient and compare with direct
differentiation of the finite product.
\end{proof}

\section{Barnes' function}

\begin{theorem}[Canonical product]
\label{thm:merged-barnes-product}
The normally convergent product
\begin{align}
 \BarnesGFunction(z+1)={}&(2\pi)^{z/2}
 \exp\!\left(-\frac{z(z+1)}2-\frac{\gamma z^2}{2}\right)             \notag\\
 &\times\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(1+\frac zk\right)^k
 \exp\!\left(-z+\frac{z^2}{2k}\right)                             \label{eq:merged-barnes-product}
\end{align}
defines an entire function satisfying
\begin{equation}
 \BarnesGFunction(1)=1,\qquad \BarnesGFunction(z+1)=\GammaFunction(z)\BarnesGFunction(z).                                \label{eq:merged-barnes-recurrence}
\end{equation}
Consequently $\BarnesGFunction(n)=1!2!\cdots(n-2)!$ for $n\geq2$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The logarithm of the $k$th factor is
$k\log(1+z/k)-z+z^2/(2k)=z^3/(3k^2)+\BigO_K(k^{-3})$ on a compact set $K$,
so the product converges normally and defines an entire function with the
prescribed integral-multiplicity zeros.  At $z=0$ it equals $1$.  Divide the
finite products at $z$ and $z-1$, rearrange before taking the limit, and use
Weierstrass' gamma product; all quadratic convergence factors cancel and the
limit is $\GammaFunction(z)$.  Normal convergence justifies passage to the limit,
proving the recurrence.  Iteration gives the integer values.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Alexeiewsky identity]
\label{thm:merged-alexeiewsky}
With compatible logarithm branches,
\begin{equation}
 \log \BarnesGFunction(z+1)=\frac z2\log(2\pi)-\frac{z(z+1)}2
 +z\log\GammaFunction(z+1)-\int_0^z\log\GammaFunction(t+1)\Differential t.                   \label{eq:merged-alexeiewsky}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Logarithmic differentiation of the product gives
\[
 \frac{\Differential}{\Differential z}\log \BarnesGFunction(z+1)
 =\frac12\log(2\pi)-z-\frac12+z\psi(z+1),
\]
where the simplification uses the digamma partial fractions.  The derivative
of the right side of \eqref{eq:merged-alexeiewsky} is identical, and both
sides vanish at $z=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
For $n\geq1$,
\begin{equation}
 \prod_{k=1}^{n}k^k=\frac{(n!)^n}{\BarnesGFunction(n+1)}.                          \label{eq:merged-hyperfactorial}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
In $(n!)^n/\BarnesGFunction(n+1)$, the exponent of $k$ is $n-(n-k)=k$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Barnes asymptotics on the positive ray]
\label{thm:merged-barnes-asymptotic}
The limit
\begin{equation}
 \log A:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left\{
 \sum_{k=1}^{n}k\log k-
 \left(\frac{n^2}{2}+\frac n2+\frac1{12}\right)\log n
 +\frac{n^2}{4}\right\}                                         \label{eq:merged-glaisher}
\end{equation}
exists and defines the Glaisher--Kinkelin constant $A$.  For every fixed
integer $p\geq1$, as $x\to+\infty$ through positive real values,
\begin{align}
 \log \BarnesGFunction(x+1)
 ={}&\frac{x^2}{2}\log x-\frac{3x^2}{4}
 +\frac{x}{2}\log(2\pi)-\frac1{12}\log x
 +\frac1{12}-\log A                                                \notag\\
 &+\sum_{k=1}^{p-1}\frac{\beta_{2k+2}}
 {2k(2k+2)x^{2k}}+\BigO_p(x^{-2p}).                                   \label{eq:merged-barnes-standard-asymptotic}
\end{align}
Equivalently, retaining the exact gamma factor,
\begin{align}
 \log \BarnesGFunction(x+1)
 ={}&\frac{x^2}{4}+x\log\GammaFunction(x+1)
 -\left(\frac{x(x+1)}2+\frac1{12}\right)\log x-\log A            \notag\\
 &+\sum_{k=1}^{p-1}\frac{\beta_{2k+2}}
 {2k(2k+1)(2k+2)x^{2k}}+\BigO_p(x^{-2p}).                             \label{eq:merged-barnes-asymptotic}
\end{align}
An empty sum is understood when $p=1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The gamma recurrence and Binet's formula \eqref{eq:binet-second} give, for
$t>0$,
\[
 \log\GammaFunction(t+1)
 =\left(t+\frac12\right)\log t-t+\frac12\log(2\pi)
 +2\int_0^\infty\frac{\arctan(x/t)}{\EulerE^{2\pi x}-1}\Differential x.
\]
For $u\geq0$, the exact finite expansion
\[
 \arctan u
 =\sum_{r=1}^{p}\frac{(-1)^{r-1}u^{2r-1}}{2r-1}
 +(-1)^p\int_0^u\frac{v^{2p}}{1+v^2}\Differential v
\]
has remainder of absolute value at most $u^{2p+1}/(2p+1)$.  Expanding
$(\EulerE^{2\pi x}-1)^{-1}$ as a geometric series and integrating termwise
gives
\[
 \int_0^\infty\frac{x^{2r-1}}{\EulerE^{2\pi x}-1}\Differential x
 =\frac{(2r-1)!\RiemannZeta(2r)}{(2\pi)^{2r}}.
\]
Together with \eqref{eq:merged-even-zeta-bernoulli}, substitution in Binet's
formula yields
\begin{equation}
 \log\GammaFunction(t+1)
 =\left(t+\frac12\right)\log t-t+\frac12\log(2\pi)
 +\sum_{r=1}^{p}\frac{\beta_{2r}}
 {2r(2r-1)t^{2r-1}}+R_p(t),
                                                                  \label{eq:merged-stirling-integrable-rem}
\end{equation}
where $R_p(t)=\BigO_p(t^{-2p-1})$.
Indeed, the arctangent remainder gives the explicit bound
\[
 |R_p(t)|\leq\frac{2}{(2p+1)t^{2p+1}}
 \int_0^\infty\frac{x^{2p+1}}{\EulerE^{2\pi x}-1}\Differential x.
\]

Split the integral in Alexeiewsky's identity
\eqref{eq:merged-alexeiewsky} at $1$, substitute
\eqref{eq:merged-stirling-integrable-rem} on $[1,x]$, and integrate the
finite elementary sum.  The term $r=1$ supplies
$-\frac1{12}\log x$ and a constant.  For $r\geq2$, the contribution of
$a_rt^{-(2r-1)}$, where
$a_r=\beta_{2r}/(2r(2r-1))$, is
\[
 xa_rx^{-(2r-1)}-\int_1^x a_rt^{-(2r-1)}\Differential t
 =\frac{\beta_{2r}}{2r(2r-2)}x^{-2r+2}+C_r.
\]
The remainder is equally controlled, but this point is worth making
explicit:
\[
 xR_p(x)-\int_1^xR_p(t)\Differential t
 =-\int_1^\infty R_p(t)\Differential t
   +xR_p(x)+\int_x^\infty R_p(t)\Differential t
 =D_p+\BigO_p(x^{-2p}).
\]
Thus the improper integral contributes only a constant and the asserted next
order.  Collecting the elementary antiderivatives and putting $k=r-1$ gives
\eqref{eq:merged-barnes-standard-asymptotic}, initially with a constant
$C_p$ in place of $1/12-\log A$.  Comparing the formulas for $p$ and $p+1$
and letting $x\to\infty$ shows $C_{p+1}=C_p$, since the extra inverse-power
term and both remainders tend to zero.  Denote the common constant by $C$.

It remains to identify $C$ without assuming the existence of the limit in
\eqref{eq:merged-glaisher}.  At a positive integer $n$, the exact recurrence
for $\BarnesGFunction$ gives
\[
 \prod_{k=1}^n k^k=\frac{(n!)^n}{\BarnesGFunction(n+1)}.
\]
Subtract the Barnes expansion with $p=1$ from $n$ times Stirling's expansion.
The result is
\[
 \sum_{k=1}^n k\log k
 =\left(\frac{n^2}{2}+\frac n2+\frac1{12}\right)\log n
  -\frac{n^2}{4}+\frac1{12}-C+\LittleO(1).
\]
Hence the limit in \eqref{eq:merged-glaisher} exists,
$\log A=1/12-C$, and so $C=1/12-\log A$.

Finally expand the exact $x\log\GammaFunction(x+1)$ in
\eqref{eq:merged-barnes-asymptotic}.  At order $x^{-2k}$ the difference
between the coefficient in
\eqref{eq:merged-barnes-standard-asymptotic} and the gamma contribution is
\[
 \frac{\beta_{2k+2}}{2k(2k+2)}
 -\frac{\beta_{2k+2}}{(2k+1)(2k+2)}
 =\frac{\beta_{2k+2}}{2k(2k+1)(2k+2)}.
\]
This proves the equivalent gamma-relative form and its remainder.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Complete hyperfactorial expansion]
\label{cor:merged-hyperfactorial-asymptotic}
For every fixed integer $p\geq1$, as $n\to\infty$ through positive integers,
\begin{align}
 \log\prod_{k=1}^n k^k
 ={}&\left(\frac{n^2}{2}+\frac n2+\frac1{12}\right)\log n
 -\frac{n^2}{4}+\log A                                             \notag\\
 &-\sum_{k=1}^{p-1}\frac{\beta_{2k+2}}
 {2k(2k+1)(2k+2)n^{2k}}+\BigO_p(n^{-2p}).                             \label{eq:merged-hyperfactorial-log-asymptotic}
\end{align}
In particular,
\begin{equation}
 \prod_{k=1}^n k^k
 =A\,n^{n^2/2+n/2+1/12}\EulerE^{-n^2/4}
 \left(1+\frac1{720n^2}+\BigO(n^{-4})\right).                         \label{eq:merged-hyperfactorial-asymptotic}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Use the exact identity
$\prod_{k=1}^n k^k=(n!)^n/\BarnesGFunction(n+1)$, insert Stirling's expansion
and \eqref{eq:merged-barnes-standard-asymptotic}, and subtract.  The
coefficient at $n^{-2k}$ is
$-\beta_{2k+2}/(2k(2k+1)(2k+2))$.  Since
$\beta_4=-1/30$, the first logarithmic correction is $1/(720n^2)$;
exponentiating changes the error only at order $n^{-4}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Complex-sector status]
The analogous expansion is classical uniformly in closed sectors
$|\arg z|\leq\pi-\delta$ \cite{NISTDLMF}, but proving that stronger statement
requires a sector-uniform complex Stirling remainder and control of the
integration path.  The positive-real theorem above is the full conclusion of
the human-readable proof supplied here; no unproved sector-uniformity is being
smuggled into its error term.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
Polynomial identities such as the Pochhammer derivative formula are formal
and require no convergence.  The partial-fraction series and Barnes product
are convergent analytic identities, while
\eqref{eq:merged-barnes-asymptotic} is a Poincaré expansion.  Keeping these
three meanings of ``equals'' separate is essential in a reliable formula
compendium.
\end{remark}

\part{Catalan Refinements and Singularity Transfer}

\chapter{Narayana numbers, polygon dissections, associahedra, and the Tamari order}
\label{ch:merged-catalan-refinements}

The earlier Lagrange chapter derived the Catalan and Fuss--Catalan formulas.
The distinct donor chapters add refinements and geometric models.
We fix the convention that $\AssociahedronByDimension{N-3}$ is the associahedron attached to a
convex $N$-gon; hence $\dim\AssociahedronByDimension{N-3}=N-3$.  This eliminates the two-unit
index shifts common in sources that index instead by dimension or number of
binary-tree leaves.

\section{Dyck paths and reflection}

A Dyck path of semilength $n$ uses $n$ up-steps $U=(1,1)$ and $n$ down-steps
$D=(1,-1)$ and never falls below height $0$.

\begin{theorem}[Catalan formula]
\label{thm:merged-catalan-reflection}
The number of Dyck paths of semilength $n$ is
\begin{equation}
 \CatalanNumber{n}=\binom{2n}{n}-\binom{2n}{n-1}
 =\frac1{n+1}\binom{2n}{n}.                                       \label{eq:merged-catalan-reflection}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Among all $\binom{2n}{n}$ balanced paths, reflect the prefix through the first
step that reaches height $-1$.  This is a bijection from bad paths to words
with $n+1$ up-steps and $n-1$ down-steps, counted by
$\binom{2n}{n-1}$.  Subtraction and simplification prove the formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Formal crosswalk]
% ed.: crosswalk added 2026-09-02.
Mathlib defines the Catalan numbers by the first-return recurrence
$\CatalanNumber{n+1}=\sum_{i+j=n}\CatalanNumber i\CatalanNumber j$ and proves
that they count Dyck words of semilength $n$
(\lean{DyckWord.card_dyckWord_semilength_eq_catalan}, through the bijection
with binary trees) and that $\CatalanNumber n=\binom{2n}{n}/(n+1)$
(\lean{catalan_eq_centralBinom_div}, \lean{succ_mul_catalan_eq_centralBinom}).
The difference form of \cref{eq:merged-catalan-reflection} is
\lean{Fabius.catalan_succ_eq_choose_sub_choose} (module
\lean{CatalanGeneratingFunction}), for $n+1$ so that no negative lower index
occurs, derived algebraically from $(n+2)\CatalanNumber{n+1}=\binom{2n+2}{n+1}$
and $\binom{2n+2}{n+1}(n+1)=\binom{2n+2}{n}(n+2)$; the reflection bijection
itself is not formalized.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[First return]
\label{thm:merged-catalan-first-return}
The generating function $C(z)=\sum \CatalanNumber{n}z^n$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
 C(z)=1+zC(z)^2,
 \qquad C(z)=\frac{1-\sqrt{1-4z}}{2z}.                              \label{eq:merged-catalan-gf}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Every nonempty path has a unique decomposition $UPDQ$, where $D$ is the first
